[06/07/26, 12:52:49 PM] Boy: What is a word?
A word is a mould.
and Meaning is what we pour into the mould
though the shape already knows.

So when paper tears,
the shape says, two things
that were one thing
are now apart.

When the eye tears,
the shape says the same thing,
but in a different voice.
Something that was whole,
now leaking, is now less contained.

A wound is what tearing leaves behind.
To wound, and to wind a thread around it,
share a shape too,
as if the hurting and the healing
were never really separate acts,
just the same word,
spoken at different times of day.

Even closeness and closing
borrow one body.
To be near someone
and to shut them out
are, apparently, spelled
the same.

Maybe this is what language is trying to teach us,
quietly underneath the dictionary, 
that separation is separation,
whether it happens to a sheet of paper
or a person,
sudden or slow,
whether we chose it
or only suffered it.

The word doesn’t care.
It holds both truths at once,
and waits for a voice
to decide which one it means.

[11/08/26, 10:42:19 PM] Boy: *MONEY FOLLOWS, MY BROTHER, MONEY FOLLOWS*
*The Philosophical Interpretation*

There are sentences that humanity spends centuries trying to understand.

*Know thyself.*

*I think, therefore I am.*

*God is dead.*

And then there is:

**“Money follows, my brother, money follows.”**

At first glance, it appears to be nothing more than a motivational statement.

Look closer, however, and we find a remarkably ancient philosophical proposition:

**Do not pursue the object of desire. Become the object worth pursuing.**

The distinction is subtle, but profound.

To chase money is to place money at the centre of one's existence. It becomes the destination, while the individual becomes merely the traveller.

But what if the relationship were reversed?

What if you stopped running after wealth and instead built something so valuable that wealth had no choice but to find you?

Suddenly, the sentence changes.

**Money follows.**

The verb matters.

Not *comes.*

Not *arrives.*

Not *is acquired.*

**Follows.**

To follow is to acknowledge another as the one who leads.

And so, hidden beneath the meme, lies an entire philosophy of ambition:

**You move. Money follows.**

The idea finds echoes across centuries.

Aristotle spoke of *telos* — the purpose toward which things move.

Nietzsche imagined the individual as someone engaged in the continuous project of overcoming the self.

Modern capitalism calls it *value creation.*

LinkedIn calls it *building your personal brand.*

Ravi Kishan, with considerably fewer words, calls it:

**“Money follows, my brother.”**

Perhaps this is the real lesson.

The person who spends their life chasing money remains perpetually behind it.

The person who pursues mastery, reputation, excellence, creation, or purpose may eventually discover something strange:

**the thing they were chasing has begun chasing them.**

Of course, this is not an argument that money magically appears when one “believes.”

Bills remain undefeated.

Capitalism remains unimpressed.

And your bank balance will not suddenly increase because you watched a motivational reel at 2 a.m.

But as a philosophy of orientation, the statement carries weight.

**Do not make wealth your identity.**

Build the identity.

Build the skill.

Build the work.

Build the reputation.

Build something worth following.

And perhaps, somewhere along the way—

**money follows.**



**Conclusion:**

We have spent 800 words trying to understand a man who simply said:

**“Money follows, my brother, money follows.”**

**Literary Society, VSSUT**
